========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, nice to meet you too. Um… the main reason I finally made the appointment is that I’ve been having this problem at work where I keep getting up from my workstation during the day, even when I know I’m supposed to stay put and finish my claim reviews. I work with insurance claims, so a lot of it is sitting there and going through things carefully, and I’ll tell myself, like, “Okay, just stay here and get through this batch,” but then I’m up again—walking away from my desk, going to get water, or just kind of moving around for no real reason.

It’s been frustrating because it’s not like I don’t understand what I’m supposed to be doing. I know the work is there, I know I need to sit and finish it, and I’ll even feel like I’m trying to make myself stay put, but I still end up leaving my seat over and over. That’s the thing that made me think, okay, I should probably talk to someone about this, because it’s starting to feel like more than just being restless or bored.

Um… I’m not really sure. I mean, I have days where I feel kind of keyed up or like I can’t settle, especially if I’ve been sitting too long, but I don’t know if I’d call it “unusually energetic” exactly. It doesn’t feel like I suddenly have all this extra energy or like I’m a totally different version of myself.

When you say wired, do you mean like, not needing sleep or feeling super confident and like everything is moving fast? Because I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a big way. I guess I’m trying to think of examples, but nothing really stands out as, like, a clear period where people would say, “Wow, you’re way more energetic than usual.” It’s more that I get restless or have trouble staying settled, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.

I’m not checking the same thing over and over, and I don’t feel a big sense of relief like, “Okay, now I can relax because I did the thing.” It’s more just… I have trouble staying there, if that makes sense.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often have trouble staying focused on tasks that require sustained mental effort, like your claim reviews?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 26 / insurance_claims_or_policy_processing_clerk / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Sustained_Mental_Effort_Avoidance\nmanifestation: \"If I see a file that needs a long written explanation or a bunch of policy language checked, I’ll put it off and do easier quick tasks until I’m basically out of time.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, definitely. That’s actually one of the biggest problems at work. If I open a claim file and I can tell it’s going to need a long written explanation, or I have to compare it against a bunch of policy language and really think through the wording, I kind of… avoid it.

Like, I’ll tell myself I’m just “warming up” by doing the easier stuff first — quick emails, smaller claims, things where I can click through and finish them fast. And then I keep doing those because they feel more manageable, and the bigger file just sits there. I know I need to do it, and I’ll keep thinking about it in the background, but I won’t actually start until I’m basically running out of time.

It’s not that I don’t understand the work, exactly. It’s more like once I know it’s going to take sustained focus, especially if I have to write out a detailed rationale or make sure I’m interpreting the policy correctly, my brain just kind of resists getting into it. Then it turns into this whole thing where I’ve spent half the day doing all these little tasks, but the main hard one is still untouched.

Yeah… looking back, I don’t think this is completely new. I’m a little fuzzy on some of the day-to-day from when I was that young, but I do remember the general pattern from elementary school, mostly because it came up in teacher comments.

They’d write things like I was bright or capable, but I’d lose worksheets, miss instructions, or not finish the classwork unless someone reminded me. It wasn’t framed like I was trying to be difficult — more like I needed a lot of prompting to stay on track and actually get the work done.

Yeah, I think so… although it’s easier for me to describe now than to remember exactly from before age 12. I can say that even now, in trainings or longer team meetings, I’m almost always doing something like bouncing my leg, clicking a pen, shifting in the chair, changing position a bunch — even when I’m really trying to look like I’m paying attention and not be distracting.

When I was younger, I don’t have as clear of a specific memory like, “this happened on this exact day,” but I do remember being kind of physically restless in class in that same way — like not necessarily running around or anything dramatic, but always needing to move a little. Tapping, messing with stuff at my desk, squirming around. It was more subtle, I guess, but it was there.

And I still catch myself doing it now because in meetings I’ll realize my leg has been bouncing the whole time, or I’ve been clicking the pen without even thinking about it, and then I’ll try to stop because I don’t want people to notice. But then a few minutes later I’m shifting around again.

I work with insurance claims, so a lot of it is sitting there and going through things carefully, and I’ll tell myself, like, “Okay, just stay here and get through this batch,” but then I’m up again—walking away from my desk, going to get water, or just kind of moving around for no real reason.\n\nIt’s been frustrating because it’s not like I don’t understand what I’m supposed to be doing. I know the work is there, I know I need to sit and finish it, and I’ll even feel like I’m trying to make myself stay put, but I still end up leaving my seat over and over. That’s the thing that made me think, okay, I should probably talk to someone about this, because it’s starting to feel like more than just being restless or bored.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually energetic or wired compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not really sure. I mean, I have days where I feel kind of keyed up or like I can’t settle, especially if I’ve been sitting too long, but I don’t know if I’d call it “unusually energetic” exactly. It doesn’t feel like I suddenly have all this extra energy or like I’m a totally different version of myself.\n\nWhen you say wired, do you mean like, not needing sleep or feeling super confident and like everything is moving fast? Because I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a big way. I guess I’m trying to think of examples, but nothing really stands out as, like, a clear period where people would say, “Wow, you’re way more energetic than usual.” It’s more that I get restless or have trouble staying settled, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you often have trouble staying seated when you were expected to?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think back to when I was a kid, and honestly the clearest version of that problem is what’s happening now at work. Like, the main thing that pushed me to come in is that I keep getting up from my workstation during the day, even when I know I’m supposed to stay put and finish my claim reviews.\n\nI don’t want to overstate the childhood part if I’m not remembering it clearly. But the “expected to stay seated and I still keep leaving my seat” thing is definitely happening now, and it’s pretty noticeable because my job is so desk-based. I’ll sit down intending to get through the work, and then somehow I’m up again even though I know I’m supposed to stay there.\n\nDoctor: Do you get up from your desk because you feel driven to check or repeat something to relieve anxiety?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. At least, that’s not how it feels to me. It’s not like I’m getting up because I have a specific thought like, “I need to go check that or something bad will happen,” or like I’m repeating a routine to calm myself down.\n\nI mean, sometimes I might get up and get water or walk over somewhere, but it doesn’t feel tied to anxiety in that way. It’s more like I realize after the fact that I’ve left my desk again, or I’m kind of looking for a reason to be up. I’m not checking the same thing over and over, and I don’t feel a big sense of relief like, “Okay, now I can relax because I did the thing.” It’s more just… I have trouble staying there, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble staying focused on tasks that require sustained mental effort, like your claim reviews?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. That’s actually one of the biggest problems at work. If I open a claim file and I can tell it’s going to need a long written explanation, or I have to compare it against a bunch of policy language and really think through the wording, I kind of… avoid it.\n\nLike, I’ll tell myself I’m just “warming up” by doing the easier stuff first — quick emails, smaller claims, things where I can click through and finish them fast. And then I keep doing those because they feel more manageable, and the bigger file just sits there. I know I need to do it, and I’ll keep thinking about it in the background, but I won’t actually start until I’m basically running out of time.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t understand the work, exactly. It’s more like once I know it’s going to take sustained focus, especially if I have to write out a detailed rationale or make sure I’m interpreting the policy correctly, my brain just kind of resists getting into it. Then it turns into this whole thing where I’ve spent half the day doing all these little tasks, but the main hard one is still untouched.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did you often have trouble paying attention or sitting still at school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… looking back, I don’t think this is completely new. I’m a little fuzzy on some of the day-to-day from when I was that young, but I do remember the general pattern from elementary school, mostly because it came up in teacher comments.\n\nThey’d write things like I was bright or capable, but I’d lose worksheets, miss instructions, or not finish the classwork unless someone reminded me. It wasn’t framed like I was trying to be difficult — more like I needed a lot of prompting to stay on track and actually get the work done.\n\nThe sitting still part I’m less sure about from before age 12, but the paying attention at school part, yeah, that was definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… although it’s easier for me to describe now than to remember exactly from before age 12. I can say that even now, in trainings or longer team meetings, I’m almost always doing something like bouncing my leg, clicking a pen, shifting in the chair, changing position a bunch — even when I’m really trying to look like I’m paying attention and not be distracting.\n\nWhen I was younger, I don’t have as clear of a specific memory like, “this happened on this exact day,” but I do remember being kind of physically restless in class in that same way — like not necessarily running around or anything dramatic, but always needing to move a little. Tapping, messing with stuff at my desk, squirming around. It was more subtle, I guess, but it was there.\n\nAnd I still catch myself doing it now because in meetings I’ll realize my leg has been bouncing the whole time, or I’ve been clicking the pen without even thinking about it, and then I’ll try to stop because I don’t want people to notice. But then a few minutes later I’m shifting around again.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I’m not totally sure on that one. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who’s constantly cutting people off, at least not in a really obvious way.

There are definitely times where I get excited or I think I know where someone is going, and I’ll start to answer a little too early, then realize they weren’t actually finished. But I don’t know if it happens “often” enough that I’d call it a major pattern. It’s more something I might notice after the fact and feel a little awkward about, like, “Oh, sorry, go ahead.”

So… maybe occasionally, but it’s not one of the main things I’ve noticed about myself.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, nice to meet you too. Um… the main reason I finally made the appointment is that I’ve been having this problem at work where I keep getting up from my workstation during the day, even when I know I’m supposed to stay put and finish my claim reviews. I work with insurance claims, so a lot of it is sitting there and going through things carefully, and I’ll tell myself, like, “Okay, just stay here and get through this batch,” but then I’m up again—walking away from my desk, going to get water, or just kind of moving around for no real reason.\n\nIt’s been frustrating because it’s not like I don’t understand what I’m supposed to be doing. I know the work is there, I know I need to sit and finish it, and I’ll even feel like I’m trying to make myself stay put, but I still end up leaving my seat over and over. That’s the thing that made me think, okay, I should probably talk to someone about this, because it’s starting to feel like more than just being restless or bored.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually energetic or wired compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not really sure. I mean, I have days where I feel kind of keyed up or like I can’t settle, especially if I’ve been sitting too long, but I don’t know if I’d call it “unusually energetic” exactly. It doesn’t feel like I suddenly have all this extra energy or like I’m a totally different version of myself.\n\nWhen you say wired, do you mean like, not needing sleep or feeling super confident and like everything is moving fast? Because I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a big way. I guess I’m trying to think of examples, but nothing really stands out as, like, a clear period where people would say, “Wow, you’re way more energetic than usual.” It’s more that I get restless or have trouble staying settled, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you often have trouble staying seated when you were expected to?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think back to when I was a kid, and honestly the clearest version of that problem is what’s happening now at work. Like, the main thing that pushed me to come in is that I keep getting up from my workstation during the day, even when I know I’m supposed to stay put and finish my claim reviews.\n\nI don’t want to overstate the childhood part if I’m not remembering it clearly. But the “expected to stay seated and I still keep leaving my seat” thing is definitely happening now, and it’s pretty noticeable because my job is so desk-based. I’ll sit down intending to get through the work, and then somehow I’m up again even though I know I’m supposed to stay there.\n\nDoctor: Do you get up from your desk because you feel driven to check or repeat something to relieve anxiety?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. At least, that’s not how it feels to me. It’s not like I’m getting up because I have a specific thought like, “I need to go check that or something bad will happen,” or like I’m repeating a routine to calm myself down.\n\nI mean, sometimes I might get up and get water or walk over somewhere, but it doesn’t feel tied to anxiety in that way. It’s more like I realize after the fact that I’ve left my desk again, or I’m kind of looking for a reason to be up. I’m not checking the same thing over and over, and I don’t feel a big sense of relief like, “Okay, now I can relax because I did the thing.” It’s more just… I have trouble staying there, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble staying focused on tasks that require sustained mental effort, like your claim reviews?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. That’s actually one of the biggest problems at work. If I open a claim file and I can tell it’s going to need a long written explanation, or I have to compare it against a bunch of policy language and really think through the wording, I kind of… avoid it.\n\nLike, I’ll tell myself I’m just “warming up” by doing the easier stuff first — quick emails, smaller claims, things where I can click through and finish them fast. And then I keep doing those because they feel more manageable, and the bigger file just sits there. I know I need to do it, and I’ll keep thinking about it in the background, but I won’t actually start until I’m basically running out of time.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t understand the work, exactly. It’s more like once I know it’s going to take sustained focus, especially if I have to write out a detailed rationale or make sure I’m interpreting the policy correctly, my brain just kind of resists getting into it. Then it turns into this whole thing where I’ve spent half the day doing all these little tasks, but the main hard one is still untouched.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did you often have trouble paying attention or sitting still at school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… looking back, I don’t think this is completely new. I’m a little fuzzy on some of the day-to-day from when I was that young, but I do remember the general pattern from elementary school, mostly because it came up in teacher comments.\n\nThey’d write things like I was bright or capable, but I’d lose worksheets, miss instructions, or not finish the classwork unless someone reminded me. It wasn’t framed like I was trying to be difficult — more like I needed a lot of prompting to stay on track and actually get the work done.\n\nThe sitting still part I’m less sure about from before age 12, but the paying attention at school part, yeah, that was definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… although it’s easier for me to describe now than to remember exactly from before age 12. I can say that even now, in trainings or longer team meetings, I’m almost always doing something like bouncing my leg, clicking a pen, shifting in the chair, changing position a bunch — even when I’m really trying to look like I’m paying attention and not be distracting.\n\nWhen I was younger, I don’t have as clear of a specific memory like, “this happened on this exact day,” but I do remember being kind of physically restless in class in that same way — like not necessarily running around or anything dramatic, but always needing to move a little. Tapping, messing with stuff at my desk, squirming around. It was more subtle, I guess, but it was there.\n\nAnd I still catch myself doing it now because in meetings I’ll realize my leg has been bouncing the whole time, or I’ve been clicking the pen without even thinking about it, and then I’ll try to stop because I don’t want people to notice. But then a few minutes later I’m shifting around again.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt other people before they’ve finished speaking?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure on that one. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who’s constantly cutting people off, at least not in a really obvious way.\n\nThere are definitely times where I get excited or I think I know where someone is going, and I’ll start to answer a little too early, then realize they weren’t actually finished. But I don’t know if it happens “often” enough that I’d call it a major pattern. It’s more something I might notice after the fact and feel a little awkward about, like, “Oh, sorry, go ahead.”\n\nSo… maybe occasionally, but it’s not one of the main things I’ve noticed about myself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates":["F90.0","F90.1","F41.9"],"note":"","is_final":false}

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.1, F41.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it's nice to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, nice to meet you too. Um… the main reason I finally made the appointment is that I’ve been having this problem at work where I keep getting up from my workstation during the day, even when I know I’m supposed to stay put and finish my claim reviews. I work with insurance claims, so a lot of it is sitting there and going through things carefully, and I’ll tell myself, like, “Okay, just stay here and get through this batch,” but then I’m up again—walking away from my desk, going to get water, or just kind of moving around for no real reason.\n\nIt’s been frustrating because it’s not like I don’t understand what I’m supposed to be doing. I know the work is there, I know I need to sit and finish it, and I’ll even feel like I’m trying to make myself stay put, but I still end up leaving my seat over and over. That’s the thing that made me think, okay, I should probably talk to someone about this, because it’s starting to feel like more than just being restless or bored.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually energetic or wired compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not really sure. I mean, I have days where I feel kind of keyed up or like I can’t settle, especially if I’ve been sitting too long, but I don’t know if I’d call it “unusually energetic” exactly. It doesn’t feel like I suddenly have all this extra energy or like I’m a totally different version of myself.\n\nWhen you say wired, do you mean like, not needing sleep or feeling super confident and like everything is moving fast? Because I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a big way. I guess I’m trying to think of examples, but nothing really stands out as, like, a clear period where people would say, “Wow, you’re way more energetic than usual.” It’s more that I get restless or have trouble staying settled, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you often have trouble staying seated when you were expected to?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think back to when I was a kid, and honestly the clearest version of that problem is what’s happening now at work. Like, the main thing that pushed me to come in is that I keep getting up from my workstation during the day, even when I know I’m supposed to stay put and finish my claim reviews.\n\nI don’t want to overstate the childhood part if I’m not remembering it clearly. But the “expected to stay seated and I still keep leaving my seat” thing is definitely happening now, and it’s pretty noticeable because my job is so desk-based. I’ll sit down intending to get through the work, and then somehow I’m up again even though I know I’m supposed to stay there.\n\nDoctor: Do you get up from your desk because you feel driven to check or repeat something to relieve anxiety?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. At least, that’s not how it feels to me. It’s not like I’m getting up because I have a specific thought like, “I need to go check that or something bad will happen,” or like I’m repeating a routine to calm myself down.\n\nI mean, sometimes I might get up and get water or walk over somewhere, but it doesn’t feel tied to anxiety in that way. It’s more like I realize after the fact that I’ve left my desk again, or I’m kind of looking for a reason to be up. I’m not checking the same thing over and over, and I don’t feel a big sense of relief like, “Okay, now I can relax because I did the thing.” It’s more just… I have trouble staying there, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble staying focused on tasks that require sustained mental effort, like your claim reviews?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. That’s actually one of the biggest problems at work. If I open a claim file and I can tell it’s going to need a long written explanation, or I have to compare it against a bunch of policy language and really think through the wording, I kind of… avoid it.\n\nLike, I’ll tell myself I’m just “warming up” by doing the easier stuff first — quick emails, smaller claims, things where I can click through and finish them fast. And then I keep doing those because they feel more manageable, and the bigger file just sits there. I know I need to do it, and I’ll keep thinking about it in the background, but I won’t actually start until I’m basically running out of time.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t understand the work, exactly. It’s more like once I know it’s going to take sustained focus, especially if I have to write out a detailed rationale or make sure I’m interpreting the policy correctly, my brain just kind of resists getting into it. Then it turns into this whole thing where I’ve spent half the day doing all these little tasks, but the main hard one is still untouched.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did you often have trouble paying attention or sitting still at school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… looking back, I don’t think this is completely new. I’m a little fuzzy on some of the day-to-day from when I was that young, but I do remember the general pattern from elementary school, mostly because it came up in teacher comments.\n\nThey’d write things like I was bright or capable, but I’d lose worksheets, miss instructions, or not finish the classwork unless someone reminded me. It wasn’t framed like I was trying to be difficult — more like I needed a lot of prompting to stay on track and actually get the work done.\n\nThe sitting still part I’m less sure about from before age 12, but the paying attention at school part, yeah, that was definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… although it’s easier for me to describe now than to remember exactly from before age 12. I can say that even now, in trainings or longer team meetings, I’m almost always doing something like bouncing my leg, clicking a pen, shifting in the chair, changing position a bunch — even when I’m really trying to look like I’m paying attention and not be distracting.\n\nWhen I was younger, I don’t have as clear of a specific memory like, “this happened on this exact day,” but I do remember being kind of physically restless in class in that same way — like not necessarily running around or anything dramatic, but always needing to move a little. Tapping, messing with stuff at my desk, squirming around. It was more subtle, I guess, but it was there.\n\nAnd I still catch myself doing it now because in meetings I’ll realize my leg has been bouncing the whole time, or I’ve been clicking the pen without even thinking about it, and then I’ll try to stop because I don’t want people to notice. But then a few minutes later I’m shifting around again.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt other people before they’ve finished speaking?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure on that one. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who’s constantly cutting people off, at least not in a really obvious way.\n\nThere are definitely times where I get excited or I think I know where someone is going, and I’ll start to answer a little too early, then realize they weren’t actually finished. But I don’t know if it happens “often” enough that I’d call it a major pattern. It’s more something I might notice after the fact and feel a little awkward about, like, “Oh, sorry, go ahead.”\n\nSo… maybe occasionally, but it’s not one of the main things I’ve noticed about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel excessively worried about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d describe myself that way, no. I mean, I worry about normal things, I think — like work stuff, money, whether I’m keeping up with responsibilities, that kind of thing — but I don’t know that it feels like this constant excessive worry about a bunch of different areas.\n\nIt’s more like if something specific is happening, I’ll think about it, maybe overthink it a bit, but I’m not walking around all day worried about everything. At least, I haven’t really noticed that as a major pattern for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so, not usually. I can talk a lot sometimes — like if it’s something I’m interested in or if I’m trying to explain myself clearly, I can probably give too much background, kind of like I’m doing now. But in situations where I’m expected to hold back, like at work or in a meeting, I’m usually pretty aware that I’m supposed to wait my turn or keep it brief.

I wouldn’t say people are constantly having to stop me or that I dominate conversations. If anything, I’m more likely to overthink whether I’m saying the right thing. There are moments where I maybe ramble a little once I do start talking, but I don’t know that I’d call it “often talking more than other people” in those kinds of situations. So… probably no, or at least not in a way that stands out to me.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, nice to meet you too. Um… the main reason I finally made the appointment is that I’ve been having this problem at work where I keep getting up from my workstation during the day, even when I know I’m supposed to stay put and finish my claim reviews. I work with insurance claims, so a lot of it is sitting there and going through things carefully, and I’ll tell myself, like, “Okay, just stay here and get through this batch,” but then I’m up again—walking away from my desk, going to get water, or just kind of moving around for no real reason.\n\nIt’s been frustrating because it’s not like I don’t understand what I’m supposed to be doing. I know the work is there, I know I need to sit and finish it, and I’ll even feel like I’m trying to make myself stay put, but I still end up leaving my seat over and over. That’s the thing that made me think, okay, I should probably talk to someone about this, because it’s starting to feel like more than just being restless or bored.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually energetic or wired compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not really sure. I mean, I have days where I feel kind of keyed up or like I can’t settle, especially if I’ve been sitting too long, but I don’t know if I’d call it “unusually energetic” exactly. It doesn’t feel like I suddenly have all this extra energy or like I’m a totally different version of myself.\n\nWhen you say wired, do you mean like, not needing sleep or feeling super confident and like everything is moving fast? Because I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a big way. I guess I’m trying to think of examples, but nothing really stands out as, like, a clear period where people would say, “Wow, you’re way more energetic than usual.” It’s more that I get restless or have trouble staying settled, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you often have trouble staying seated when you were expected to?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think back to when I was a kid, and honestly the clearest version of that problem is what’s happening now at work. Like, the main thing that pushed me to come in is that I keep getting up from my workstation during the day, even when I know I’m supposed to stay put and finish my claim reviews.\n\nI don’t want to overstate the childhood part if I’m not remembering it clearly. But the “expected to stay seated and I still keep leaving my seat” thing is definitely happening now, and it’s pretty noticeable because my job is so desk-based. I’ll sit down intending to get through the work, and then somehow I’m up again even though I know I’m supposed to stay there.\n\nDoctor: Do you get up from your desk because you feel driven to check or repeat something to relieve anxiety?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. At least, that’s not how it feels to me. It’s not like I’m getting up because I have a specific thought like, “I need to go check that or something bad will happen,” or like I’m repeating a routine to calm myself down.\n\nI mean, sometimes I might get up and get water or walk over somewhere, but it doesn’t feel tied to anxiety in that way. It’s more like I realize after the fact that I’ve left my desk again, or I’m kind of looking for a reason to be up. I’m not checking the same thing over and over, and I don’t feel a big sense of relief like, “Okay, now I can relax because I did the thing.” It’s more just… I have trouble staying there, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble staying focused on tasks that require sustained mental effort, like your claim reviews?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. That’s actually one of the biggest problems at work. If I open a claim file and I can tell it’s going to need a long written explanation, or I have to compare it against a bunch of policy language and really think through the wording, I kind of… avoid it.\n\nLike, I’ll tell myself I’m just “warming up” by doing the easier stuff first — quick emails, smaller claims, things where I can click through and finish them fast. And then I keep doing those because they feel more manageable, and the bigger file just sits there. I know I need to do it, and I’ll keep thinking about it in the background, but I won’t actually start until I’m basically running out of time.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t understand the work, exactly. It’s more like once I know it’s going to take sustained focus, especially if I have to write out a detailed rationale or make sure I’m interpreting the policy correctly, my brain just kind of resists getting into it. Then it turns into this whole thing where I’ve spent half the day doing all these little tasks, but the main hard one is still untouched.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did you often have trouble paying attention or sitting still at school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… looking back, I don’t think this is completely new. I’m a little fuzzy on some of the day-to-day from when I was that young, but I do remember the general pattern from elementary school, mostly because it came up in teacher comments.\n\nThey’d write things like I was bright or capable, but I’d lose worksheets, miss instructions, or not finish the classwork unless someone reminded me. It wasn’t framed like I was trying to be difficult — more like I needed a lot of prompting to stay on track and actually get the work done.\n\nThe sitting still part I’m less sure about from before age 12, but the paying attention at school part, yeah, that was definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… although it’s easier for me to describe now than to remember exactly from before age 12. I can say that even now, in trainings or longer team meetings, I’m almost always doing something like bouncing my leg, clicking a pen, shifting in the chair, changing position a bunch — even when I’m really trying to look like I’m paying attention and not be distracting.\n\nWhen I was younger, I don’t have as clear of a specific memory like, “this happened on this exact day,” but I do remember being kind of physically restless in class in that same way — like not necessarily running around or anything dramatic, but always needing to move a little. Tapping, messing with stuff at my desk, squirming around. It was more subtle, I guess, but it was there.\n\nAnd I still catch myself doing it now because in meetings I’ll realize my leg has been bouncing the whole time, or I’ve been clicking the pen without even thinking about it, and then I’ll try to stop because I don’t want people to notice. But then a few minutes later I’m shifting around again.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt other people before they’ve finished speaking?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure on that one. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who’s constantly cutting people off, at least not in a really obvious way.\n\nThere are definitely times where I get excited or I think I know where someone is going, and I’ll start to answer a little too early, then realize they weren’t actually finished. But I don’t know if it happens “often” enough that I’d call it a major pattern. It’s more something I might notice after the fact and feel a little awkward about, like, “Oh, sorry, go ahead.”\n\nSo… maybe occasionally, but it’s not one of the main things I’ve noticed about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel excessively worried about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d describe myself that way, no. I mean, I worry about normal things, I think — like work stuff, money, whether I’m keeping up with responsibilities, that kind of thing — but I don’t know that it feels like this constant excessive worry about a bunch of different areas.\n\nIt’s more like if something specific is happening, I’ll think about it, maybe overthink it a bit, but I’m not walking around all day worried about everything. At least, I haven’t really noticed that as a major pattern for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk more than other people in situations where you’re expected to hold back?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not usually. I can talk a lot sometimes — like if it’s something I’m interested in or if I’m trying to explain myself clearly, I can probably give too much background, kind of like I’m doing now. But in situations where I’m expected to hold back, like at work or in a meeting, I’m usually pretty aware that I’m supposed to wait my turn or keep it brief.\n\nI wouldn’t say people are constantly having to stop me or that I dominate conversations. If anything, I’m more likely to overthink whether I’m saying the right thing. There are moments where I maybe ramble a little once I do start talking, but I don’t know that I’d call it “often talking more than other people” in those kinds of situations. So… probably no, or at least not in a way that stands out to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

Not really, no. I mean, I don’t think I have that feeling of being, like, physically wound up all the time or like I have to keep pacing around even when I want to relax.

I can get restless in a normal way sometimes, like if I’ve been sitting for a long time or I’m waiting around with nothing to do, but I wouldn’t describe it as being “driven by a motor.” That sounds more intense than what I experience. If I’m relaxing, I can usually sit still or lie down without feeling like I have to keep moving. So I don’t think that one really fits me, at least not in a noticeable way.

I work with insurance claims, so a lot of it is sitting there and going through things carefully, and I’ll tell myself, like, “Okay, just stay here and get through this batch,” but then I’m up again—walking away from my desk, going to get water, or just kind of moving around for no real reason.\n\nIt’s been frustrating because it’s not like I don’t understand what I’m supposed to be doing. I know the work is there, I know I need to sit and finish it, and I’ll even feel like I’m trying to make myself stay put, but I still end up leaving my seat over and over. That’s the thing that made me think, okay, I should probably talk to someone about this, because it’s starting to feel like more than just being restless or bored.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually energetic or wired compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not really sure. I mean, I have days where I feel kind of keyed up or like I can’t settle, especially if I’ve been sitting too long, but I don’t know if I’d call it “unusually energetic” exactly. It doesn’t feel like I suddenly have all this extra energy or like I’m a totally different version of myself.\n\nWhen you say wired, do you mean like, not needing sleep or feeling super confident and like everything is moving fast? Because I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a big way. I guess I’m trying to think of examples, but nothing really stands out as, like, a clear period where people would say, “Wow, you’re way more energetic than usual.” It’s more that I get restless or have trouble staying settled, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you often have trouble staying seated when you were expected to?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think back to when I was a kid, and honestly the clearest version of that problem is what’s happening now at work. Like, the main thing that pushed me to come in is that I keep getting up from my workstation during the day, even when I know I’m supposed to stay put and finish my claim reviews.\n\nI don’t want to overstate the childhood part if I’m not remembering it clearly. But the “expected to stay seated and I still keep leaving my seat” thing is definitely happening now, and it’s pretty noticeable because my job is so desk-based. I’ll sit down intending to get through the work, and then somehow I’m up again even though I know I’m supposed to stay there.\n\nDoctor: Do you get up from your desk because you feel driven to check or repeat something to relieve anxiety?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. At least, that’s not how it feels to me. It’s not like I’m getting up because I have a specific thought like, “I need to go check that or something bad will happen,” or like I’m repeating a routine to calm myself down.\n\nI mean, sometimes I might get up and get water or walk over somewhere, but it doesn’t feel tied to anxiety in that way. It’s more like I realize after the fact that I’ve left my desk again, or I’m kind of looking for a reason to be up. I’m not checking the same thing over and over, and I don’t feel a big sense of relief like, “Okay, now I can relax because I did the thing.” It’s more just… I have trouble staying there, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble staying focused on tasks that require sustained mental effort, like your claim reviews?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. That’s actually one of the biggest problems at work. If I open a claim file and I can tell it’s going to need a long written explanation, or I have to compare it against a bunch of policy language and really think through the wording, I kind of… avoid it.\n\nLike, I’ll tell myself I’m just “warming up” by doing the easier stuff first — quick emails, smaller claims, things where I can click through and finish them fast. And then I keep doing those because they feel more manageable, and the bigger file just sits there. I know I need to do it, and I’ll keep thinking about it in the background, but I won’t actually start until I’m basically running out of time.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t understand the work, exactly. It’s more like once I know it’s going to take sustained focus, especially if I have to write out a detailed rationale or make sure I’m interpreting the policy correctly, my brain just kind of resists getting into it. Then it turns into this whole thing where I’ve spent half the day doing all these little tasks, but the main hard one is still untouched.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did you often have trouble paying attention or sitting still at school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… looking back, I don’t think this is completely new. It wasn’t framed like I was trying to be difficult — more like I needed a lot of prompting to stay on track and actually get the work done.\n\nThe sitting still part I’m less sure about from before age 12, but the paying attention at school part, yeah, that was definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… although it’s easier for me to describe now than to remember exactly from before age 12. I can say that even now, in trainings or longer team meetings, I’m almost always doing something like bouncing my leg, clicking a pen, shifting in the chair, changing position a bunch — even when I’m really trying to look like I’m paying attention and not be distracting.\n\nWhen I was younger, I don’t have as clear of a specific memory like, “this happened on this exact day,” but I do remember being kind of physically restless in class in that same way — like not necessarily running around or anything dramatic, but always needing to move a little. Tapping, messing with stuff at my desk, squirming around. It was more subtle, I guess, but it was there.\n\nAnd I still catch myself doing it now because in meetings I’ll realize my leg has been bouncing the whole time, or I’ve been clicking the pen without even thinking about it, and then I’ll try to stop because I don’t want people to notice. It’s more like I realize after the fact that I’ve left my desk again, or I’m kind of looking for a reason to be up. I’m not checking the same thing over and over, and I don’t feel a big sense of relief like, “Okay, now I can relax because I did the thing.” It’s more just… I have trouble staying there, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble staying focused on tasks that require sustained mental effort, like your claim reviews?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. That’s actually one of the biggest problems at work. If I open a claim file and I can tell it’s going to need a long written explanation, or I have to compare it against a bunch of policy language and really think through the wording, I kind of… avoid it.\n\nLike, I’ll tell myself I’m just “warming up” by doing the easier stuff first — quick emails, smaller claims, things where I can click through and finish them fast. And then I keep doing those because they feel more manageable, and the bigger file just sits there. I know I need to do it, and I’ll keep thinking about it in the background, but I won’t actually start until I’m basically running out of time.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t understand the work, exactly. It’s more like once I know it’s going to take sustained focus, especially if I have to write out a detailed rationale or make sure I’m interpreting the policy correctly, my brain just kind of resists getting into it. Then it turns into this whole thing where I’ve spent half the day doing all these little tasks, but the main hard one is still untouched.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did you often have trouble paying attention or sitting still at school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… looking back, I don’t think this is completely new. I’m a little fuzzy on some of the day-to-day from when I was that young, but I do remember the general pattern from elementary school, mostly because it came up in teacher comments.\n\nThey’d write things like I was bright or capable, but I’d lose worksheets, miss instructions, or not finish the classwork unless someone reminded me. It wasn’t framed like I was trying to be difficult — more like I needed a lot of prompting to stay on track and actually get the work done.\n\nThe sitting still part I’m less sure about from before age 12, but the paying attention at school part, yeah, that was definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… although it’s easier for me to describe now than to remember exactly from before age 12. I can say that even now, in trainings or longer team meetings, I’m almost always doing something like bouncing my leg, clicking a pen, shifting in the chair, changing position a bunch — even when I’m really trying to look like I’m paying attention and not be distracting.\n\nWhen I was younger, I don’t have as clear of a specific memory like, “this happened on this exact day,” but I do remember being kind of physically restless in class in that same way — like not necessarily running around or anything dramatic, but always needing to move a little. Tapping, messing with stuff at my desk, squirming around. It was more subtle, I guess, but it was there.\n\nAnd I still catch myself doing it now because in meetings I’ll realize my leg has been bouncing the whole time, or I’ve been clicking the pen without even thinking about it, and then I’ll try to stop because I don’t want people to notice. But then a few minutes later I’m shifting around again.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt other people before they’ve finished speaking?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure on that one. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who’s constantly cutting people off, at least not in a really obvious way.\n\nThere are definitely times where I get excited or I think I know where someone is going, and I’ll start to answer a little too early, then realize they weren’t actually finished. But I don’t know if it happens “often” enough that I’d call it a major pattern. It’s more something I might notice after the fact and feel a little awkward about, like, “Oh, sorry, go ahead.”\n\nSo… maybe occasionally, but it’s not one of the main things I’ve noticed about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel excessively worried about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d describe myself that way, no. And then I keep doing those because they feel more manageable, and the bigger file just sits there. It’s more like once I know it’s going to take sustained focus, especially if I have to write out a detailed rationale or make sure I’m interpreting the policy correctly, my brain just kind of resists getting into it. Then it turns into this whole thing where I’ve spent half the day doing all these little tasks, but the main hard one is still untouched.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did you often have trouble paying attention or sitting still at school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… looking back, I don’t think this is completely new. I’m a little fuzzy on some of the day-to-day from when I was that young, but I do remember the general pattern from elementary school, mostly because it came up in teacher comments.\n\nThey’d write things like I was bright or capable, but I’d lose worksheets, miss instructions, or not finish the classwork unless someone reminded me. It wasn’t framed like I was trying to be difficult — more like I needed a lot of prompting to stay on track and actually get the work done.\n\nThe sitting still part I’m less sure about from before age 12, but the paying attention at school part, yeah, that was definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… although it’s easier for me to describe now than to remember exactly from before age 12. I can say that even now, in trainings or longer team meetings, I’m almost always doing something like bouncing my leg, clicking a pen, shifting in the chair, changing position a bunch — even when I’m really trying to look like I’m paying attention and not be distracting.\n\nWhen I was younger, I don’t have as clear of a specific memory like, “this happened on this exact day,” but I do remember being kind of physically restless in class in that same way — like not necessarily running around or anything dramatic, but always needing to move a little. Tapping, messing with stuff at my desk, squirming around. It was more subtle, I guess, but it was there.\n\nAnd I still catch myself doing it now because in meetings I’ll realize my leg has been bouncing the whole time, or I’ve been clicking the pen without even thinking about it, and then I’ll try to stop because I don’t want people to notice. But then a few minutes later I’m shifting around again.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt other people before they’ve finished speaking?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure on that one. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who’s constantly cutting people off, at least not in a really obvious way.\n\nThere are definitely times where I get excited or I think I know where someone is going, and I’ll start to answer a little too early, then realize they weren’t actually finished. But I don’t know if it happens “often” enough that I’d call it a major pattern. It’s more something I might notice after the fact and feel a little awkward about, like, “Oh, sorry, go ahead.”\n\nSo… maybe occasionally, but it’s not one of the main things I’ve noticed about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel excessively worried about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d describe myself that way, no. I mean, I worry about normal things, I think — like work stuff, money, whether I’m keeping up with responsibilities, that kind of thing — but I don’t know that it feels like this constant excessive worry about a bunch of different areas.\n\nIt’s more like if something specific is happening, I’ll think about it, maybe overthink it a bit, but I’m not walking around all day worried about everything. At least, I haven’t really noticed that as a major pattern for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk more than other people in situations where you’re expected to hold back?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not usually. I can talk a lot sometimes — like if it’s something I’m interested in or if I’m trying to explain myself clearly, I can probably give too much background, kind of like I’m doing now. But in situations where I’m expected to hold back, like at work or in a meeting, I’m usually pretty aware that I’m supposed to wait my turn or keep it brief.\n\nI wouldn’t say people are constantly having to stop me or that I dominate conversations. If anything, I’m more likely to overthink whether I’m saying the right thing. There are moments where I maybe ramble a little once I do start talking, but I don’t know that I’d call it “often talking more than other people” in those kinds of situations. So… probably no, or at least not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you’re driven by a motor, as if you have to keep moving even when you want to relax?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I don’t think I have that feeling of being, like, physically wound up all the time or like I have to keep pacing around even when I want to relax.\n\nI can get restless in a normal way sometimes, like if I’ve been sitting for a long time or I’m waiting around with nothing to do, but I wouldn’t describe it as being “driven by a motor.” That sounds more intense than what I experience. If I’m relaxing, I can usually sit still or lie down without feeling like I have to keep moving. So I don’t think that one really fits me, at least not in a noticeable way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble waiting your turn?\n\nPatient: I don’t think that’s a big one for me, no. Like, if I’m in line somewhere or waiting for someone else to finish talking, I might feel a little impatient internally, especially if it’s taking longer than I expected, but I don’t usually act on it or cut in or anything like that.\n\nI’m generally able to wait my turn. I might kind of check the time or mentally drift off while I’m waiting, but I wouldn’t say I have a persistent problem with waiting my turn. It’s not something people complain about with me, at least not that I can think of."
  }

Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation. Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.     "S006": "The patient explicitly reports avoiding work tasks that require sustained mental effort, instead doing easier tasks until time is running out."
  }
}

Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.
